Unipotent Ideals for Spin and Exceptional Groups
Lucas Mason-Brown, Dmytro Matvieievskyi

Abstract
. In the monograph [ LMM21 ] , we define the notion of a unipotent representation of a complex reductive group. The representations we define include, as a proper subset, all special unipotent representations in the sense of [ BV85 ] and form the (conjectural) building blocks of the unitary dual. In [ LMM21 ] we provide combinatorial formulas for the infinitesimal characters of all unipotent representations of linear classical groups. In this paper, we establish analogous formulas for spin and exceptional groups, thus completing the determination of the infinitesimal characters of all unipotent ideals. Using these formulas, we prove an old conjecture of Vogan: all unipotent ideals are maximal. For G G a real reductive Lie group (not necessarily complex), we introduce the notion of a unipotent representation attached to a rigid nilpotent orbit (in the complexified Lie algebra of G G ). Like their complex group counterparts, these representations form the (conjectural) building blocks of the unitary dual. Using the atlas software (and the work of [ AMLV ] ) we show that if G G is a real form of a simple group of exceptional type, all such representations are unitary.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
17B35, 17B08, 22E46

1.
Introduction

Let
G
be a complex reductive algebraic group and let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
. Inspired by the orbit method for nilpotent and solvable Lie groups, Vogan proposes the following in
[
Vog90
]
:

Conjecture 1.0.1
.

For each (finite connected) cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
of a nilpotent co-adjoint
G
-orbit, there is a canonically defined completely prime primitive ideal
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
in the universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
.

The properties of the ideal
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
and its relation to
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
are described in some detail in
[
Vog90
]
and also in
[
Vog87
, Chp 9]
. In particular, the ideals
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
should be maximal and should include, as a proper subset, all special unipotent ideals in the sense of
[
BV85
]
. Conjecture
1.0.1
is called the
quantization problem for nilpotent covers
and the conjectured ideals are called
unipotent
. We note that Conjecture
1.0.1
is intimately related to the problem of classifying unitary representations. If we can define unipotent ideals in
U(\mathfrak{g})
, we can define unipotent representations (of the complex group
G
)—a
G
-representation is
unipotent
if it is irreducible and annihilated (on both sides) by a unipotent ideal. It is conjectured in
[
Vog87
, Chp 9]
that unipotent representations are unitary, and in fact form the ‘building blocks’ of the unitary dual.

Conjecture
1.0.1
has generated a large body of research over the past several decades, with contributions from Barbasch (
[
Bar89
]
), Vogan (
[
Vog86
]
,
[
Vog88
]
,
[
Vog90
]
), Joseph (
[
Jos76
]
), McGovern (
[
McG94
]
), Brylinski (
[
Bry03
]
), and others. Previous approaches have made use of specialized constructions to resolve Conjecture
1.0.1
in certain special cases (e.g. for
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
equal to the
minimal nilpotent orbit
, as in
[
Jos76
]
, or for
G
equal to a linear classical group, as in
[
Bar89
]
,
[
McG94
]
, and
[
Bry03
]
). In the monograph
[
LMM21
]
, we give a systematic solution to Conjecture
1.0.1
in all cases, using the theory of filtered quantizations of conical symplectic singularities. Our definition of
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
will be recalled in Section
4
.

Write
\mathfrak{z}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
for the center of
U(\mathfrak{g})
. If
I\subset U(\mathfrak{g})
is a primitive ideal, then
I\cap\mathfrak{z}(U(\mathfrak{g}))
is the kernel of an algebra homomorphism
\mathfrak{z}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{C}
, called the
infinitesimal character
of
I
. If we fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, we can use the Harish-Chandra isomorphism
\mathfrak{z}(U(\mathfrak{g}))\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W}
to identify infinitesimal characters with
W
-orbits on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. Write
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W
for the infinitesimal character of the unipotent ideal
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. One drawback of the approach taken in
[
LMM21
]
is that the infinitesimal character
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is not easy to determine from the definition of
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
. The computation of
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
requires a detailed analysis of the birational geometry of
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
. In
[
LMM21
]
, we deduce combinatorial formulas for all
\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
in the case of linear classical groups. In this paper, we establish analogous formulas for spin and exceptional groups, thus completing the computation of all unipotent ideals.

We will now provide a more detailed overview of the results contained in this paper. One of the crucial ideas herein is
birational induction
. This is an operation which takes nilpotent covers for a Levi subgroup
L\subset G
to nilpotent covers for
G
. A nilpotent cover is called
birationally rigid
if it cannot be obtained via birational induction from a proper Levi subgroup. In Sections
3.8
and
3.9
, we give a classification of birationally rigid nilpotent covers for simple exceptional groups, see Propositions
3.8.3
and
3.9.5
.

One important property of unipotent ideals is that their infinitesimal characters are preserved under birational induction. More precisely, if
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{G}
is birationally induced from
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
, then

(1.0.1)

\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{G})=\gamma_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}).

In view of (
1.0.1
), it is enough to compute the infinitesimal characters attached to birationally rigid covers. For simple exceptional groups, we provide in Section
4.3
a complete list of such infinitesimal characters (see Tables
4
-
13
). For spin groups, we provide in Section
4.2
a combinatorial formula (see Proposition
4.2.6
).

These computations allow us to complete the proof of the following conjecture of Vogan (see
[
Vog87
, Conj 9.18]
).

Theorem 1.0.2

(See Theorem
5.0.1
below)
.

Let
G
be a complex connected reductive algebraic group and let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a (finite connected) covering of a nilpotent co-adjoint
G
-orbit. Then
I_{0}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
is a maximal ideal in
U(\mathfrak{g})
.

In Section
6
, we investigate the applicability of these ideas to real reductive groups. Suppose
G
is a real reductive Lie group (not necessarily linear) and let
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a rigid nilpotent orbit for the complexified Lie algebra of
G
. Let
K\subset G
be a maximal compact subgroup. We propose the following definition.

Definition 1.0.3

(See Definition
6.0.1
below)
.

A unipotent representation of
G
attached to
\mathbb{O}
is an irreducible
(\mathfrak{g},K)
-module
\mathcal{B}
such that
\Ann_{U(\mathfrak{g})}(\mathcal{B})=I_{0}(\mathbb{O})
.

If
G
is a real form of a simple exceptional group and
\mathbb{O}
is a special nilpotent orbit, then it will be shown in
[
AMLV
]
that all such representations are unitary. In Section
6.1
, we do the same for non-special orbits, proving the following result.

Theorem 1.0.4

(See Theorem
6.1.1
below)
.

Suppose
G
is a real form of a simple exceptional group and
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a rigid nilpotent orbit. Then all unipotent representations attached to
\mathbb{O}
are unitary.

The proof of this result is a straightforward (albeit time-consuming) computation using the
atlas
software.

The methods used in this paper mostly belong to the theory of filtered quantizations of conical symplectic singularities. We review what is needed from this theory in Section
2
. The reader who is not interested in our methods may prefer to regard this paper as a sort of ‘treasure map’ for interesting unitary representations of spin and exceptional groups. Many of the representations we describe have never been studied in the literature.

1.1.
Acknowledgements

The authors would like to thank Ivan Losev and David Vogan for many helpful conversations. Many of the computations in this paper were assisted, and in some cases wholly carried out, using the
atlas
software. We would like to acknowledge the whole
atlas
team, especially Jeffrey Adams, Annegret Paul, Marc van Leeuwen, and David Vogan, for creating and maintaining such fantastically useful software. The work of D.M. was partially supported by the NSF
under grant DMS-2001139.

2.
Quantizations of conical symplectic singularities

In this section, we review the theory of filtered quantizations of conical symplectic singularities. The results in this section come from a variety of sources, including
[
Bea00
]
,
[
Kal06
]
,
[
Los16
]
, and
[
LMM21
]
. Our exposition roughly follows Section 4 of
[
LMM21
]
.

2.1.
Filtered quantizations

Let
A
be a
graded Poisson algebra
of degree
-d\in\mathbb{Z}_{<0}
. By this, we will mean a finitely-generated commutative associative unital algebra equipped with two additional structures: an algebra grading

A=\bigoplus_{i=-\infty}^{\infty}A_{i}

and a Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
of degree
-d

\{A_{i},A_{j}\}\subset A_{i+j-d},\qquad i,j\in\mathbb{Z}.

For any algebra of this form, one can define the notion of a
filtered quantization
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

A
filtered quantization
of
A
is a pair
(\mathcal{A},\theta)
consisting of

(i)

an associative algebra
\mathcal{A}
equipped with a complete and separated filtration by subspaces

\mathcal{A}=\bigcup_{i=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{\leq i},\qquad...\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\leq-1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\leq 0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\leq 1}\subseteq...

such that

[\mathcal{A}_{\leq i},\mathcal{A}_{\leq j}]\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\leq i+j-d}\qquad i,j\in\mathbb{Z},

and

(ii)

an isomorphism of graded Poisson algebras

\theta:\gr(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\sim}A,

where the Poisson bracket on
\gr(\mathcal{A})
is defined by

\{a+\mathcal{A}_{\leq i-1},b+\mathcal{A}_{\leq j-1}\}=[a,b]+\mathcal{A}_{\leq i+j-d-1},\qquad a\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq i},\ b\in\mathcal{A}_{\leq j}.

An isomorphism of filtered quantizations
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\theta_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{A}_{2},\theta_{2})
is an isomorphism of filtered algebras
\phi:\mathcal{A}_{1}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}\circ\gr(\phi)
. Denote the set of isomorphism classes of quantizations of
A
by
\mathrm{Quant}(A)
.

Often, the isomorphism
\theta
is clear from the context, and will be omitted from the notation. However, the reader should keep in mind that a filtered quantization
(\mathcal{A},\theta)
is
not
determined up to isomorphism by
\mathcal{A}
alone.

Now, suppose
G
is an algebraic group which acts rationally on
A
by graded Poisson automorphisms. Write
\mathrm{Der}(A)
for the Lie algebra of derivations. The
G
-action on
A
induces by differentiation a Lie algebra homomorphism

\mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Der}(A),\qquad\xi\mapsto\xi_{A},

We say that
A
is
Hamiltonian
if there is a
G
-equivariant map
\varphi:\mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}A_{d}
(called a
classical co-moment map
) such that

\{\varphi(\xi),a\}=\xi_{A}(a),\qquad\xi\in\mathfrak{g},\quad a\in A.

A filtered quantization
(\mathcal{A},\theta)
is
G
-
equivariant
if
G
acts rationally on
\mathcal{A}
by filtered algebra automorphisms and the isomorphism
\theta:\gr(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\sim}A
is
G
-equivariant. In this setting (as above) we get a Lie algebra homomorphism

\mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Der}(\mathcal{A}),\qquad\xi\mapsto\xi_{\mathcal{A}}.

Definition 2.1.2
.

Suppose
A
is a graded Poisson algebra equipped with a Hamiltonian
G
-action. A
Hamiltonian
quantization of
A
is a triple
(\mathcal{A},\theta,\Phi)
consisting of

(i)

a
G
-equivariant filtered quantization
(\mathcal{A},\theta)
of
A
, and

(ii)

a
G
-equivariant map
\Phi:\mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{\leq d}
(called a
quantum co-moment map
) such that

[\Phi(\xi),a]=\xi_{\mathcal{A}}(a),\qquad\xi\in\mathfrak{g},\quad a\in\mathcal{A}.

An isomorphism
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\theta_{1},\Phi_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{A}_{2},\theta_{2},\Phi_{2})
of Hamiltonian quantizations of
A
is a
G
-equivariant isomorphism of filtered algebras
\phi:\mathcal{A}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}\circ\gr(\phi)
and
\Phi_{2}=\phi\circ\Phi_{1}
. Denote the set of isomorphism classes of Hamiltonian quantizations of
A
by
\mathrm{Quant}^{G}(A)
.

2.2.
Conical symplectic singularities

Let
X
be a normal Poisson variety.

Definition 2.2.1

(
[
Bea00
]
, Def 1.1)
.

We say that
X
has
symplectic singularities
if

(i)

the regular locus
X^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset X
is symplectic; denote the symplectic form by
\omega^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

(ii)

there is a resolution of singularities
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
such that
\rho^{*}(\omega^{\mathrm{reg}})
extends to a regular (not necessarily symplectic)
2
-form on
Y
.

In this paper, we will consider symplectic singularities of a very special type. Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
as in Section
2.1
.

Definition 2.2.2
.

A
conical symplectic singularity
is a normal affine Poisson variety
X
with symplectic singularities and a contracting rational
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action such that
\mathbb{C}[X]
is a graded Poisson algebra of degree
-d
.

Example 2.2.3
.

The following are examples of conical symplectic singularities

(i)

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Sp}(2)
be a finite subgroup. Then the Kleinian singularity
\Sigma=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
is a conical symplectic singularity, see
[
Bea00
, Prop 2.4]
. For
\rho
we take the minimal resolution
\mathfrak{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Sigma
.

(ii)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex reductive Lie algebra and let
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a nilpotent orbit. Then
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
is a conical symplectic singularity, see
[
Bea00
, Sec 2.5]
.

(iii)

In the setting of
(ii)
, let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
be a connected finite étale cover. Then
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
is a conical symplectic singularity, see
[
Los21
, Lem 2.5]
.

For an arbitrary variety
X
, define the subvarieties
X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},...
as follows:
X_{0}:=X
and
X_{k+1}:=X_{k}-X_{k}^{\mathrm{reg}}
. If
X
is Poisson, then all
X_{k}
are Poisson subvarieties of
X
.

Definition 2.2.4
.

We say that
X
has finitely many (symplectic) leaves if
X_{k}^{\mathrm{reg}}
is a symplectic variety for all
k
. By a symplectic leaf of
X
we mean an irreducible (i.e. connected) component of
X_{k}^{\mathrm{reg}}
for some
k
.

Proposition 2.2.5

(Thm 2.3,
[
Kal06
]
)
.

Suppose
X
has symplectic singularities. Then
X
has finitely many leaves.

2.3.
Namikawa space

Let
X
be a normal Poisson variety with symplectic singularities. Recall that a normal variety
Y
is
\mathbb{Q}
-
factorial
if every Weil divisor has a (nonzero) integer multiple which is Cartier. The following is a consequence of
[
BCHM10
]
(see
[
Los19
, Prop 2.1]
for a proof).

Proposition 2.3.1
.

There is a birational projective morphism
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
such that

(i)

Y
is an irreducible, normal, Poisson variety (in particular,
Y
has symplectic singularities).

(ii)

Y
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial.

(iii)

Y
has terminal singularities.

Remark 2.3.2
.

Modulo (i), (iii) is equivalent to the condition that the singular locus of
Y
is of codimension
\geq 4
, see
[
Nam01
, Main Thm]
. In practice, the latter condition is often easier to check.

The map
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
in the proposition above (or the variety
Y
itself, if the map is understood) is called a
\mathbb{Q}
-
factorial terminalization
. If
X
is conical, then
Y
admits a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action such that
\rho
is
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant, see
[
Nam08
, A.7]
.

Example 2.3.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex reductive Lie algebra and let
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be its nilpotent cone. By Example
2.2.3
(ii) (and the normality of
\mathcal{N})
,
X:=\mathcal{N}
is a conical symplectic singularity. For
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
we take the Springer resolution
T^{*}(G/B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
.

Definition 2.3.4
.

Let
X
be a conical symplectic singularity and
Y
a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization of
X
.
The
Namikawa space
associated to
X
is the complex vector space

\mathfrak{P}^{X}:=H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C}).

Remark 2.3.5
.

It was shown in
[
LMM21
]
that
\mathfrak{P}^{X}
depends only on
X
up to canonical isomorphism (i.e. it is independent of the choice of
\mathbb{Q}
-terminalization), see
[
LMM21
, Lem 4.6.6]
and the discussion following it. In particular, the notation
\mathfrak{P}^{X}
is justified.

2.4.
Structure of Namikawa space

Following
[
Nam11
]
and
[
Los16
]
, we will provide a description of
\mathfrak{P}^{X}
in terms of the geometry of
X
. For this discussion, it is convenient to fix a
\mathbb{Q}
-terminalization
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
. For each codimension 2 leaf
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset X
, the formal slice to
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset X
is identified with the formal neighborhood at
0
in a Kleinian singularity
\Sigma_{k}=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma_{k}
, see
[
Nam11
]
. Under the McKay correspondence,
\Gamma_{k}
corresponds to a complex simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{k}
of type A, D, or E. Fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{k}
. Write
\Lambda_{k}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
for the weight lattice and
W_{k}
for the Weyl group. If we choose a point
x\in\mathfrak{L}_{k}
, there is a natural identification
H^{2}(\rho^{-1}(x),\mathbb{Z})\simeq\Lambda_{k}
, and
\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})
acts on
\Lambda_{k}
by diagram automorphisms. The
partial Namikawa space
corresponding to
\mathfrak{L}_{k}
is the subspace

\mathfrak{P}^{X}_{k}:=(\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}.

The embedding
\rho^{-1}(x)\subset Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
induces a map on cohomology

\mathfrak{P}^{X}:=H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}H^{2}(\rho^{-1}(x),\mathbb{C})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}\simeq(\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}=:\mathfrak{P}^{X}_{k}.

Also, define

\mathfrak{P}^{X}_{0}:=H^{2}(X^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C}).

The embedding
X^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset Y^{\mathrm{reg}}
induces a map on cohomology

\mathfrak{P}^{X}:=H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}H^{2}(X^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})=:\mathfrak{P}^{X}_{0}.

Proposition 2.4.1

(
[
Los16
]
, Lem 2.8)
.

The maps
\mathfrak{P}^{X}\rightarrow\mathfrak{P}^{X}_{k}
assemble into a linear isomorphism

(2.4.1)

\mathfrak{P}^{X}\simeq\bigoplus_{k=0}^{t}\mathfrak{P}^{X}_{k},\qquad\lambda\mapsto(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{t}).

Finally, we define the
Namikawa Weyl group
of
X
. The
\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})
-action on
\Lambda_{k}
induces a
\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})
-action on
W_{k}
. Consider the subgroup
W_{k}^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}\subset W_{k}
. There is a natural action of
W_{k}^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}
on
\mathfrak{P}^{X}_{k}=(\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}
. The
Namikawa Weyl group
associated to
X
is the product

W:=\prod_{k}W_{k}^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}.

We let
W
act on
\mathfrak{P}
via the isomorphism (
2.4.1
) (the action on
\mathfrak{P}^{X}_{0}
is trivial).

2.5.
Finite covers of conical symplectic singularities

Let
X
be a conical symplectic singularity. In this section, we will define the notion of a
finite cover
of
X
. Let
p^{\prime}:\widetilde{X}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X^{\mathrm{reg}}
be a finite étale cover of the regular locus
X^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset X
. Rescaling if necessary, we can arrange so that the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
lifts to
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
. Consider the composition
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}\overset{p^{\prime}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}X^{\mathrm{reg}}\hookrightarrow X
and its Stein factorization
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{reg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

Note that
\widetilde{X}
is affine and
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
embeds into
\widetilde{X}
as an open subvariety. Since
\operatorname{codim}(X^{\mathrm{sing}},X)\geqslant 2
and
p:\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
is finite, we have that
\codim(\widetilde{X}-\widetilde{X}^{\prime},\widetilde{X})\geq 2
. Thus the algebra
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}^{\prime}]
is finitely generated and

\widetilde{X}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{X}^{\prime}])
. In particular, the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
extends to
\widetilde{X}
. In fact,
\widetilde{X}
is a conical symplectic singularity, see
[
Los21
, Lemma 2.5]
. A map
p:\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
obtained in this fashion is called a
finite cover
of
X
. A finite cover
p:\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
is
Galois
if it is Galois over the regular locus
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

For each codimension 2 leaf
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset X
, choose a system of fundamental weights

\omega_{1}(k),\omega_{2}(k),...,\omega_{n(k)}(k)\in\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}

To each fundamental weight
\omega_{i}(k)
we assign a coefficient
a_{i}
, which is the multiplicity of the corresponding simple root in the highest weight for
\mathfrak{g}_{k}
(in type
A
,
a_{i}=1
for every
i
, and this is the only case that will concern us). Define the element

\epsilon_{k}:=|\Gamma_{k}|^{-1}\sum_{i}a_{i}\omega_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}.

Lemma 2.5.1

(Prop 5.3.1,
[
LMM21
]
)
.

Suppose
X
admits a finite Galois cover
\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
such that
\widetilde{X}
has no codimension 2 leaves. Then for each
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset X
, the element
\epsilon_{k}
is a fixed point for
\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})
and hence an element of the partial Namikawa space
\mathfrak{P}_{k}=(\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}
.

In the setting of Lemma
2.5.1
, define

(2.5.1)

\epsilon:=(0,\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},...,\epsilon_{t})\in\bigoplus_{i}\mathfrak{P}^{X}_{i}\simeq\mathfrak{P}^{X}

We call
\epsilon
the
weighted barycenter parameter
for
X
. We note that the Galois group
\Pi
of
\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
acts on the canonical quantization
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}
, and
(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}})^{\Pi}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}^{X}
, see
[
LMM21
, Prop 5.3.1]
.

2.6.
Filtered quantizations of conical symplectic singularities

In this section, we will recall the classification of filtered quantizations of conical symplectic singularities. Let
X
be a conical symplectic singularity and choose a
\mathbb{Q}
-terminalization
\rho:Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
. For any graded smooth symplectic variety
V
, there is a (non-commutative)
period map

\mathrm{Per}:\mathrm{Quant}(V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}H^{2}(V,\mathbb{C}),

see
[
BK04
, Sec 4]
,
[
Los12
, Sec 2.3]
.

Proposition 2.6.1

(
[
Los16
]
, Prop 3.1(1))
.

The maps

\mathrm{Quant}(Y)\overset{|_{Y^{\mathrm{reg}}}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\mathrm{Quant}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}})\overset{\mathrm{Per}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}H^{2}(Y^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}

are bijections.

For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{P}
, let
\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}
denote the corresponding filtered quantization of
Y
and let
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}:=\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{D}_{\lambda})
.

Theorem 2.6.2

(Prop 3.3, Thm 3.4,
[
Los16
]
)
.

The following are true:

(i)

For every
\lambda\in\mathfrak{P}
, the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
is a filtered quantization of
\mathbb{C}[X]
.

(ii)

Every filtered quantization of
\mathbb{C}[X]
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\mathfrak{P}
.

(iii)

For every
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
if and only if
\lambda^{\prime}\in W\cdot\lambda
.

Hence, the map
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
induces a bijection

\mathfrak{P}/W\simeq\mathrm{Quant}(\mathbb{C}[X]),\qquad W\cdot\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}.

There is an equivariant version of this result, which we will now state. Let
G
be a connected reductive algebraic group and suppose
A:=\mathbb{C}[X]
admits a Hamiltonian
G
-action, see Section
2.1
. Define the
extended Namikawa space

\overline{\mathfrak{P}}^{X}:=\mathfrak{P}^{X}\oplus\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}

This space should be viewed as an equivariant version of
\mathfrak{P}^{X}
. Let
W^{X}
act on
\overline{\mathfrak{P}}^{X}
via the decomposition above (the
W
-action on the second factor is defined to be trivial).

Proposition 2.6.3

(Lem 4.11.2,
[
LMM21
]
)
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive algebraic group and suppose
A:=\mathbb{C}[X]
admits a Hamiltonian
G
-action. Then the following are true:

(i)

There is a unique classical co-moment map
\varphi:\mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}A_{d}
.

(ii)

Every filtered quantization
\mathcal{A}\in\mathrm{Quant}(A)
has a unique
G
-equivariant structure.

(iii)

For every
\mathcal{A}\in\mathrm{Quant}(A)
and
\chi\in\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}
, there is a unique quantum co-moment map
\Phi_{\chi}:\mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{\leq d}
such that
\Phi|_{\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})}=\chi
.

In particular, there is a canonical bijection

\overline{\mathfrak{P}}^{X}/W^{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Quant}^{G}(A)\qquad W^{X}(\lambda,\chi)\mapsto(\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{X},\Phi_{\chi}).

One consequence of this proposition is that there is always a distinguished quantization of
X
.

Definition 2.6.4

(Def 5.0.1,
[
LMM21
]
)
.

The
canonical Hamiltonian quantization
of
\mathbb{C}[X]
is the Hamiltonian quantization corresponding to the parameter
0\in\overline{\mathfrak{P}}^{X}
.

3.
Nilpotent orbits and covers

In this section, we collect some basic facts about nilpotent orbits and their (finite connected) covers.

3.1.
Classification of nilpotent orbits and covers

In classical types, nilpotent orbits are classified by (decorated) integer partitions.

Definition 3.1.1
.

A partition
p
is of
type C
(resp type
B/D
) if every odd part (resp. even part) occurs with even multiplicity.

The following result is well-known.

Proposition 3.1.2

(Section 5.1,
[
CM93
]
)
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}
is classical. The set of nilpotent orbits
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is parameterized by (decorated) partitions as follows

(a)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
, the set of nilpotent orbits is in one-to-one correspondence with partitions of
n
.

(b)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
, the set of nilpotent orbits is in one-to-one correspondence with partitions of
2n+1
of type B/D.

(c)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
, the set of nilpotent orbits is in one-to-one correspondence with partitions of
2n
of type C.

(d)

If
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
, the set of nilpotent orbits is in one-to-one correspondence with partitions of
2n
of type B/D, except that each
very even
partition (i.e. a partition containing only even parts) corresponds to two nilpotent orbits, labeled
\mathbb{O}^{I}
and
\mathbb{O}^{II}
.

If
p
is a partition of the appropriate type, we will denote the corresponding nilpotent orbit by
\mathbb{O}_{p}
. In exceptional types, we will use the Bala-Carter classification to label nilpotent orbits, see
[
CM93
, Sec 8.4]
for an explanation.

By a ‘nilpotent cover’ we will mean a finite connected
G
-equivariant cover of a nilpotent co-adjoint orbit. Up to isomorphism, nilpotent covers of
\mathbb{O}
are paramterized by conjugacy classes of subgroups of the (necessarily finite)
G
-equivariant fundamental group
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
. A description of these fundamental groups can be found in
[
CM93
, Sec 6.1]
(for classical groups) and
[
CM93
, Sec 8.4]
(for simply connected exceptional groups). We will occasionally need a description of
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
for
more general classes of groups (for example, the Levi subgroups of simply connected exceptional groups, which need not be simply connected). In these cases, we use the
atlas
software to compute
\pi_{1}^{G}(\mathbb{O})
.

3.2.
Geometry of nilpotent orbits and covers

In this section, we will collect some basic facts about the geometry of nilpotent orbits and covers. Suppose
\mathbb{O}
is a nilpotent orbit. The singular locus of
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
coincides with the boundary
\partial\mathbb{O}=\overline{\mathbb{O}}\setminus\mathbb{O}
, see
[
Nam04
, Proof of Prop 2.2]
. Let
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
be a maximal
G
-orbit in
\partial\mathbb{O}
. For each point
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
, there is a transverse slice
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
to
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
(obtained, for example, by intersecting the Slodowy slice to
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
at
e^{\prime}
with
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
). The variety
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
has an isolated singularity at
e^{\prime}
and a natural rational
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action which is contracting onto
e^{\prime}
, see
[
GG02
, Sec 4]
. We note that
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is independent, up to algebraic isomorphism, of the choice of
e^{\prime}
as well as the
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime},h^{\prime})
used to define the Slodowy slice. We call
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
the
singularity
of the orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. The varieties
S_{\mathbb{O}^{\prime},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
were described in classical types by Kraft and Procesi in
[
KP82
]
and in exceptional types by Fu, Juteau, Levy, and Sommers in
[
FJLS15
]
.

In this paper, we will restrict our attention to the singularities corresponding to codimension 2 orbits
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, i.e. to
dimension 2 singularities
in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. The dimension 2 singularities in nilpotent orbit closures can be rather complicated (sometimes non-normal) varieties. We will briefly recall some of the standard conventions for denoting them, from
[
Slo80
]
,
[
KP82
]
and
[
FJLS15
]
. Fix a dimension 2 singularity
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
. If
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is normal, it is isomorphic to a Kleinian singularity of type
A
,
D
, or
E
. As explained in Section
2.4
, the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
acts on the Dynkin diagram of
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
by a finite group
K
of diagram automorphisms. Following
[
Slo80
]
and
[
FJLS15
]
, the pair
(S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}},K)
is denoted by

•

B_{k}
, if
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is of type
A_{2k-1}
, and
K=S_{2}
,

•

C_{k}
, if
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is of type
D_{k+1}
, and
K=S_{2}
,

•

F_{4}
, if
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is of type
E_{6}
, and
K=S_{2}
,

•

G_{2}
, if
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is of type
D_{4}
, and
K=S_{3}
,

•

A_{2k}^{+}
, if
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is of type
A_{2k}
, and
K=S_{2}
.

If
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is
non-normal
and
\mathfrak{g}
is a classical Lie algebra, then
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is of the following type:

•

nA_{k}
:
n
copies of the Kleinian singularity of type
A_{k}
, meeting at the singular point.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is exceptional, there are several additional non-normal singularities which can appear. In the notation of
[
FJLS15
]
, they are:

•

nD_{k}
:
n
copies of the Kleinian singularity of type
D_{k}
, meeting at the singular point.

•

m
: a non-normal 2-dimensional conical singularity admitting an
\mathrm{SL}(2)
-action with an open orbit isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
and normalization isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, see
[
FJLS15
, Section 1.8.4]
for details.

•

\mu
: a non-normal 2-dimensional conical singularity with normalization isomorphic to a Kleinian singularity of type
A_{3}
, see
[
FJLS15
, Sec 1.8.4]
for details.

The next lemma, which asserts that the above singularities are in fact the only possibilities, is immediate from
[
KP82
]
and
[
FJLS15
]
.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
be a codimension 2 orbit, and assume that the singularity
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is non-normal. Then
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is of type
nA_{k}
,
nD_{k}
(for some
n
,
k
),
m
, or
\mu
.

Now let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a (finite connected) cover of
\mathbb{O}
and let
\widetilde{X}=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
. Recall, (iii) of Example
2.2.3
, that
\widetilde{X}
is a conical symplectic singularity. Let
\mu:\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
denote the moment map. If
\mathfrak{L}\subset\widetilde{X}
is a codimension 2 leaf, then
\mu(\overline{\mathfrak{L}})
is the closure of a codimension 2 orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, see
[
LMM21
, Lem 4.6.1]
. This defines a map

(3.2.1)

\{\text{codimension 2 leaves in }\widetilde{X}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\text{codimension 2 orbits in }\overline{\mathbb{O}}\}

Let
\Sigma
denote the Kleinian singularity corresponding to the leaf
\mathfrak{L}\subset\widetilde{X}
, see Section
2.4
. There is a closed embedding
\Sigma\subset\widetilde{X}
, constructed as follows. Since
S_{\mathbb{O}^{\prime},\mathbb{O}}
is transverse to
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
,
\mu^{-1}(S_{\mathbb{O}^{\prime},\mathbb{O}})
is transverse to every leaf in
\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
. Thanks to the contracting
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
S_{\mathbb{O}^{\prime},\mathbb{O}}
,
\mu^{-1}(S_{\mathbb{O}^{\prime},\mathbb{O}})
splits into a disjoint union of connected components, indexed by points in
\mu^{-1}(S_{\mathbb{O}^{\prime},\mathbb{O}}\cap\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
. Choose a point in this set lying in
\mathfrak{L}
and let
\Sigma
be its connected component in
\mu^{-1}(S_{\mathbb{O}^{\prime},\mathbb{O}})
. Then
\Sigma
is a Kleinian singularity and
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\Sigma]^{\wedge})
is a formal slice to
\mathfrak{L}
.

If we specialize to the case of nilpotent orbits, the map (
3.2.1
) is almost always a bijection.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Suppose
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathbb{O}
is a nilpotent orbit. Then the following are true:

(i)

The map (
3.2.1
) is injective.

(ii)

The map (
3.2.1
) is surjective unless
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
contains a dimension 2 singularity of type
m
.

Proof.

If
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
is normal in codimension 2, then
\mu
is an isomorphism over every codimension 2 orbit in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. Hence, (
3.2.1
) is a bijection.

If
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
is a codimension 2 orbit,
\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
is a (union of) codimension 2 orbits. Furthermore, by
3.2.1
,
\mu^{-1}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})
lies in the singular locus of
X
if and only if the singularity
S_{\mathbb{O},\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
is not of type
m
. This proves (ii) in all cases. To prove (i) we consider all orbits
\mathbb{O}
such that
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
contains a non-normal dimension 2 singularity not of type
m
. The general idea is that if the singularity corresponds to a partial Namikawa space of dimension
t
, then there are at most
[\frac{\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}}{t}]
leaves with this singularity.

(i)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a classical rank-
n
Lie algebra not of type
A
. Suppose that
\mathbb{O}_{1}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
corresponds to a minimal degeneration of type (e) in the sense of
[
KP82
, Table 1]
, and let
\alpha
and
\beta
be the corresponding partitions. Let
\mathbb{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{O}_{t}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
be the codimension 2 orbits, and let
k
be the largest integer such that
\alpha_{k}>\beta_{k}
. By loc.cit.,
\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{k+1}+2t
for some
t
. Consider the Levi subalgebra
\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(k)^{\times t}\times\mathfrak{g}(n-2kt)
. Let
\mathbb{O}_{L}\subset\mathfrak{l}^{*}
be the nilpotent orbit corresponding to the partition
\gamma
given by
\gamma_{i}=\alpha_{i}-2t
for
i\leq k
, and
\gamma_{i}=\alpha_{i}
for
i>k
. Set
\mathfrak{P}_{L}=\mathfrak{P}^{X_{L}}
. We note that
\mathbb{O}
is birationally induced from
(L,\mathbb{O}_{L})
, and for each
j\neq 2
there is a codimension
2
orbit
\mathbb{O}_{L,j}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
such that
\mathbb{O}_{j}
is birationally induced from
\mathbb{O}_{L,j}
. By
[
Mat20
, Lemma 4.16]
, the singularities of
\mathbb{O}_{L,j}
in
X_{L}
and of
\mathbb{O}_{j}
in
X
are equivalent, and
\dim\mathfrak{P}_{j}^{X}=\dim\mathfrak{P}_{j}^{X_{L}}
. We have
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X_{L}}+t
, and therefore
\dim\mathfrak{P}_{1}^{X}=t
. It follows that there is only one codimension
2
leaf in
X
over the orbit
\mathbb{O}_{1}
.

(ii)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
F_{4}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=C_{3}(a_{1})
. There is one codimension
2
orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=B_{2}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularity is of type
2A_{1}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=1
, and therefore there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
.

(iii)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
F_{4}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=C_{3}
. There is one codimension
2
orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=F_{4}(a_{1})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularity is of type
4G_{2}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=2
, and therefore there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
.

(iv)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
E_{6}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=A_{4}
. There is one codimension
2
orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=D_{4}(a_{1})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularity is of type
3C_{2}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=3
, and therefore there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
.

(v)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
E_{7}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=D_{6}(a_{1})
. There are two codimension
2
orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1}=E_{7}(a_{5})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
and
\mathbb{O}_{2}=D_{5}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularities are of type
3C_{2}
and
A_{1}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=4
, and therefore there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}_{1}
.

(vi)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
E_{7}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=A_{3}+A_{2}
. There is one codimension
2
orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{1}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularity is of type
2A_{1}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=1
, and therefore there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
.

(vii)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
E_{7}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{1}
. There are two codimension
2
orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1}=D_{4}(a_{1})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
and
\mathbb{O}_{2}=A_{3}+2A_{1}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularities are of type
3A_{1}
and
A_{1}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=2
, and therefore there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}_{1}
.

(viii)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
E_{8}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=E_{7}(a_{1})
. There is one codimension
2
orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=E_{8}(b_{5})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularity is of type
3C_{5}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=5
, and therefore there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
.

(ix)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
E_{8}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=E_{7}(a_{1})
. There are two codimension
2
orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1}=D_{6}(a_{1})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
and
\mathbb{O}_{2}=A_{6}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularities are of type
2A_{1}
and
A_{1}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=2
. Since
\dim\mathfrak{P}_{2}^{X}=1
, it implies that there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}_{1}
.

(x)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
E_{8}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=D_{6}(a_{1})
. There are two codimension
2
orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1}=E_{8}(a_{7})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
and
\mathbb{O}_{2}=D_{5}+A_{1}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularities are of type
10G_{2}
and
A_{1}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=3
. Since
\dim\mathfrak{P}_{2}^{X}=1
, there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}_{1}
.

(xi)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
E_{8}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=A_{6}
. There is one codimension
2
orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=E_{8}(a_{7})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularity is of type
5G_{2}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=2
, and therefore there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
.

(xii)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
E_{8}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=E_{7}(a_{5})
. There are two codimension
2
orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1}=D_{6}(a_{2})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
and
\mathbb{O}_{2}=E_{6}(a_{3})+A_{1}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularities are of types
2A_{1}
and
m
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=1
, and therefore there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}_{1}
.

(xiii)

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be of type
E_{8}
, and set
\mathbb{O}=A_{3}+A_{2}
. There is one codimension
2
orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{1}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, and the corresponding singularity is of type
3A_{1}
. Using
[
DE09
, Tables]
,
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=1
, and therefore there is one codimension
2
leaf over
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
.

∎

Remark 3.2.3
.

Let
\mathbb{O}_{k}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
be a codimension 2 orbit such that the corresponding singularity is not of type
m
. Then by Lemma
3.2.2
, there is a unique codimension 2 leaf
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
which maps to
\mathbb{O}_{k}
under (
3.2.1
).

3.3.
Induction of nilpotent orbits

Let
M\subset G
be a Levi subgroup, and let
\mathbb{O}_{M}
be a nilpotent
M
-orbit. Fix a parabolic subgroup
Q\subset G
with a Levi decomposition
Q=MU
. The annihilator of
\mathfrak{q}
in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a
Q
-stable subspace
\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Choosing a nondegenerate invariant symmetric form on
\mathfrak{g}
, we get a
Q
-invariant identification
\mathfrak{q}^{\perp}\simeq\mathfrak{u}
. Form the
G
-equivariant fiber bundle
G\times^{Q}(\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})
over the partial flag variety
G/Q
. There is a proper
G
-equivariant map

\mu:G\times^{Q}(\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\qquad\mu(g,\xi)=\Ad^{*}(g)\xi

The image of
\mu
is a closed irreducible
G
-invariant subset of
\mathcal{N}
, and hence the closure in
\mathcal{N}
of a nilpotent
G
-orbit, denoted
\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. The correspondence

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{M}:\{\text{nilpotent }M\text{-orbits}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{\text{nilpotent }G\text{-orbits}\}

is called
Lusztig-Spaltenstein
induction. A nilpotent orbit is
rigid
if it cannot be induced from a proper Levi subgroup.

Proposition 3.3.1

(
[
LS79
]
or
[
CM93
]
, Sec 7)
.

Lusztig-Spaltenstein induction has the following properties

(i)

\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{M}
depends only on
M
(and not on
Q
)

(ii)

If
L\subset M
is a Levi subgroup of
M
, then

\Ind^{G}_{L}=\Ind^{G}_{M}\circ\Ind^{M}_{L}.

(iii)

If
\mathbb{O}
is a nilpotent orbit, there is a Levi sugroup
M\subset G
and a rigid nilpotent
M
-orbit
\mathbb{O}_{M}
such that

\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}

(iv)

If
\mathbb{O}_{M}\subset\mathfrak{m}^{*}
is a nilpotent
M
-orbit and
\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
, then

\mathrm{codim}(\mathbb{O}_{M},\mathcal{N}_{M})=\codim(\mathbb{O},\mathcal{N}).

In classical types, a classification of rigid nilpotent orbits and a description of induction in terms of partitions can be found in
[
CM93
, Sec 7.3]
. In exceptional types, this information can be found in the tables appearing in
[
DE09
, Sec 4]
. For the explicit computations in Sections
3.9
and
4.3
, we will make repeated use of these descriptions.

3.4.
Birational induction of nilpotent covers

Choose a Levi subgroup
M\subset G
, a nilpotent
M
-orbit
\mathbb{O}_{M}
, and a (finite, connected)
M
-equivariant cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
of
\mathbb{O}_{M}
. Let
\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M}
. Consider the affine variety
\widetilde{X}_{M}:=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}])
. There is an
M
-action on
\widetilde{X}_{M}
(induced from the
M
-action on
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
) and a finite surjective
M
-equivariant map
\widetilde{X}_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
. Let
\widetilde{\mu}
denote the composition

G\times^{Q}(\widetilde{X}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G\times^{Q}(\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})\overset{\mu}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}.

Note that
\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}(\mathbb{O})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
is a (finite, connected)
G
-equivariant cover. The correspondence

\displaystyle\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}:\{M\text{-eqvt nilpotent covers}\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\{G\text{-eqvt nilpotent covers}\}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}(\mathbb{O})

is called
birational induction
. A nilpotent cover is
birationally rigid
if it cannot be birationally induced from a proper Levi subgroup.

The main properties of birational induction are catalogued below.

Proposition 3.4.1

(Prop 2.4.1,
[
LMM21
]
)
.

Birational induction has the following properties

(i)

\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}
depends only on
M
(and not on
Q
)

(ii)

If
L\subset M
is a Levi subgroup of
M
, then

\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\circ\mathrm{Bind}^{M}_{L}.

(iii)

If
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a
G
-equivariant nilpotent cover, there is a Levi subgroup
M\subset G
and a birationally rigid
M
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
such that

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}.

The pair
(M,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
is called a
birationally minimal induction datum
and is unique up to conjugation by
G
.

(iv)

If we write
\deg(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
for the degree of the covering map
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}_{M}
, then

\deg(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})\text{ divides }\deg(\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})).

3.5.
Filtered quantizations of nilpotent covers

Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a nilpotent cover and consider the affine variety
\widetilde{X}:=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
. By (iii) of Example
2.2.3
,
\widetilde{X}
is a conical symplectic singularity. Fix the notation of Section
2.4
, i.e.
\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}
,
W^{\widetilde{X}}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{k}^{\widetilde{X}}
, and so on. By Theorem
2.6.2
, there is a canonical bijection

(3.5.1)

\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}/W^{\widetilde{X}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Quant}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])\qquad W^{\widetilde{X}}\cdot\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{A}^{\widetilde{X}}_{\lambda}

In this section, we will re-interpret the spaces
\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{k}^{\widetilde{X}}
in terms of purely Lie-theoretic information.

Fix a birationally minimal induction datum
(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
for
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
(cf. Proposition
3.4.1
(iii)) and let
\widetilde{X}_{L}:=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}])
. Choose a parabolic subgroup
P\subset G
with Levi factor
L
and consider the map

\widetilde{\mu}:\widetilde{Y}:=G\times^{P}(\widetilde{X}_{L}\times\mathfrak{p}^{\perp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}

defined in Section
3.4
. Since
\widetilde{\mu}^{-1}\simeq\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
,
\widetilde{\mu}
factors through a partial resolution

(3.5.2)

\rho:\widetilde{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\widetilde{X}.

Proposition 3.5.1
.

The following are true:

(i)

The map (
3.5.2
) is a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization.

(ii)

There is a linear isomorphism

\eta:\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{2}(G/P,\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{2}(\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})=:\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}},

where the second map is the pullback along the natural projection
\widetilde{Y}^{\mathrm{reg}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}G/P.

(iii)

W^{\widetilde{X}}
is identified with a normal subgroup of
N_{G}(L)/L
, with its canonical action on
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])
.

(iv)

Up to the action of
W^{\widetilde{X}}
on the target, the map
\eta:\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}
is independent of the choice of parabolic
P
.

Proof.

(i) is
[
Mat20
, Cor 4.3]
. For orbits, (ii) and (iii) are
[
Los16
, Prop 4.7]
. The proofs there can be easily generalized to arbitrary nilpotent covers. (iv) is
[
LMM21
, Prop 7.2.5]
.
∎

Combining (
3.5.1
) and (ii) of Proposition
3.5.1
, we obtain a natural bijection

\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])/W^{\widetilde{X}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Quant}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])\qquad W^{\widetilde{X}}\cdot\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{A}^{\widetilde{X}}_{\eta(\lambda)}

Note that
G
acts on
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
by graded Poisson automorphisms. There is a classical co-moment map
\varphi:\mathfrak{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}]
obtained from the map of varieties
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. The map
\eta:\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}
extends to an isomorphism (still denoted by
\eta
)

\eta:\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}])\oplus\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}\oplus\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g})^{*}=\overline{\mathfrak{P}}^{\widetilde{X}}.

So by Proposition
2.6.3
, we obtain a natural bijection

\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Quant}^{G}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])\qquad W^{\widetilde{X}}\cdot(\lambda,\chi)\mapsto(\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{\widetilde{X}},\Phi_{\chi}^{\widetilde{X}}).

3.6.
Description of partial Namikawa spaces

In this seciton, we will give a Lie-theoretic description of the partial Namikawa spaces
\mathfrak{P}_{k}^{\widetilde{X}}
(under some conditions). Passing to a covering group if necessary, we can (and will) assume that
G
is simply connected.
Let
R_{x}
denote the reductive part of the stabilizer of
x\in\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
and let
\mathfrak{r}
be its Lie algebra. We note that
\mathfrak{r}
does not depend on the choice of a point
x
, and the adjoint action of
R_{x}
on
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{r})
factors through
R_{x}/R_{x}^{\circ}\simeq\pi^{G}_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})
.

Lemma 3.6.1
.

The following are true:

(i)

Restriction along
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}\subset\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{reg}}
induces a linear isomorphism

\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\widetilde{X}}=H^{2}(\widetilde{X}^{\mathrm{reg}},\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\mathbb{C})

(ii)

There is a natural identification

H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{r})^{\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})}

A description of
\mathfrak{r}
can be found in
[
CM93
, Sec 6.1]
(for classical types) and
[
Car93
, Sec 13.1]
(for exceptional types).

Remark 3.6.2
.

If
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is the
universal
cover of
\mathbb{O}
, then
H^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}},\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{r})
by Lemma
3.6.1
. In particular,
H^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}},\mathbb{C})=0
if and only if
\mathfrak{r}
is semisimple. On the other hand, if
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathbb{O}
, then
H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{r})^{\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})}
was computed in every case by Biswas and Chatterjee in
[
BC12
]
.

Assume for the remainder of this subsection that
H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\mathbb{C})=0
and fix a birationally minimal induction datum
(L,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L})
for
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
. Suppose
Q\subset G
is a parabolic subgroup with Levi factor
M
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
is an
M
-equivariant nilpotent cover with
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
. The triple
(Q,M,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
gives rise to a projective birational morphism (generalizing the map (
3.5.2
))

(3.6.1)

\rho:G\times^{Q}(\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}])\times\mathfrak{q}^{\perp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])

Proposition 3.6.3

(Prop 7.5.6,
[
LMM21
]
)
.

For each codimension 2 leaf
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset\widetilde{X}
, there is a unique pair
(M_{k},\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M_{k}})
consisting of a Levi subgroup
M_{k}\subset G
and a
M_{k}
-equivariant nilpotent cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M_{k}}
such that

(i)

L\subset M_{k}
.

(ii)

\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M_{k}}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M_{k}}
.

(iii)

For any parabolic
Q\subset G
with Levi factor
M_{k}
, the partial resolution (
3.6.1
) resolves
\Sigma_{k}
and preserves
\Sigma_{j}
for
j\neq k
.

The pair
(M_{k},\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M_{k}})
appearing in Proposition
3.6.3
is called the
\mathfrak{L}_{k}
-
adapted resolution datum.

Proposition 3.6.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}_{k}\subset\widetilde{X}
be a codimension 2 leaf and let
(M_{k},\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M_{k}})
be the
\mathfrak{L}_{k}
-adapted resolution datum. Then the following are true:

(i)

The closed embedding
\Sigma_{k}\subset\widetilde{X}
(cf. Section
3.2
) lifts to a closed embedding
\mathfrak{S}_{k}\subset\widetilde{Z}_{k}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{L}
is a line bundle on
\widetilde{Z}_{k}
, then
\mathcal{L}|_{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}
is a
\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})
-equivariant line bundle on
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, i.e. there is a restriction map

(3.6.2)

\Pic(\widetilde{Z}_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Pic(\mathfrak{S}_{k})^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}

(iii)

There are natural group isomorphisms

\Pic(\widetilde{Z}_{k})\simeq\mathfrak{X}(M_{k}),\qquad\Pic(\mathfrak{S}_{k})\simeq\Lambda_{k},

i.e. (
3.6.2
) induces a group homomorphism

(3.6.3)

\mathfrak{X}(M_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Lambda_{k}^{\pi_{1}(\mathfrak{L}_{k})}

(iv)

The complexification of (
3.6.3
) is a linear isomorphism

(3.6.4)

\eta_{k}:\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{m}_{k})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{P}_{k}^{\widetilde{X}},

(v)

The following diagram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{P}^{\widetilde{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{m}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{P}_{k}^{\widetilde{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{k}}

Proof.

(i) follows from (iii) of Proposition
3.6.3
. (iii) follows from
[
LMM21
, Prop 7.1.2]
. (ii), (iv), and (v) follow from
[
LMM21
, Lem 7.5.7]
.
∎

The isomorphism
\eta_{k}
was computed in
[
LMM21
, Sec 7.7]
under certain conditions on
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{k}
. The relevant statements will be recalled in Section
3.10.3
.

3.7.
Geometric characterization of birationally rigid covers

Combining Proposition
2.4.1
and (ii) of Proposition
3.5.1
, we obtain the following (purely geometric) characterization of birational rigidity.

Proposition 3.7.1
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
be a nilpotent cover. Then
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid if and only if the following conditions hold:

(i)

H^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}},\mathbb{C})=0
.

(ii)

\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}])
has no codimension 2 leaves.

Checking condition (i) of Proposition
3.7.1
is usually easy in view of Lemma
3.6.1
. Checking condition (ii) is a subtler business in general. We will develop some techniques for doing so in Section
3.9
.

3.8.
Classification of birationally rigid orbits

Let
\mathbb{O}
be a nilpotent orbit.

Proposition 3.8.1
.

Let
\mathbb{O}
be a nilpotent orit. Then
\mathbb{O}
is birationally rigid if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

H^{2}(\mathbb{O},\mathbb{C})=0
.

(ii)

All dimension 2 singularities in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
are of type
m
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.2.2
, condition (ii) above is equivalent to condition (ii) of Proposition
3.7.1
. Now Proposition
3.8.1
follows at once from Proposition
3.7.1
.
∎

An advantage of this formulation is that condition (ii) above is very easy to check. In classical types,
there are no singularities of type
m
. So (ii) is equivalent to the condition that there are no codimension 2 orbits in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. The set of codimension 2 orbits in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
was described by Kraft and Procesi in
[
KP82
]
in terms of the partition corresponding to
\mathbb{O}
. From this description, one easily deduces the following.

Proposition 3.8.2

(Prop 7.6.3,
[
LMM21
]
)
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{g}
is classical and let
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a nilpotent orbit corresponding to a partition
p
. Then
\mathbb{O}
is birationally rigid if and only if one of the following is true:

(i)

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}(n)
and
\mathbb{O}=\{0\}
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n+1)
or
\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
and
p
satisfies

p_{i}\leq p_{i+1}+1\qquad\forall i.

(iii)

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(2n)
,
p
satisfies

p_{i}\leq p_{i+1}+1\qquad\forall i,

and
p
is not of the form
(2^{m},1^{2})
for some
m
.

In exceptional types, condition (ii) of Proposition
3.8.1
can be checked by inspecting the incidence tables in
[
FJLS15
, Sec 13]
. One easily arrives at the following classification.

Proposition 3.8.3
.

The following is a complete list of birationally rigid orbits in simple exceptional Lie algebras:

\mathfrak{g}

Birationally rigid orbits

G_{2}

\{0\}
,
A_{1}
,
\widetilde{A}_{1}

F_{4}

\{0\}
,
A_{1}
,
\widetilde{A}_{1}
,
A_{1}+\widetilde{A}_{1}
,
A_{2}+\widetilde{A}_{1}
,
\widetilde{A}_{2}+A_{1}

E_{6}

\{0\}
,
A_{1}
,
3A_{1}
,
2A_{2}+A_{1}

E_{7}

\{0\}
,
A_{1}
,
2A_{1}
,
(3A_{1})^{\prime}
,
4A_{1}
,
A_{2}+A_{1}
,
A_{2}+2A_{1}
,
2A_{1}+A_{1}
,
(A_{3}+A_{1})^{\prime}
,
A_{4}+A_{1}

E_{8}

\{0\}
,
A_{1}
,
2A_{1}
,
3A_{1}
,
4A_{1}
,
A_{2}+A_{1}
,
A_{2}+2A_{1}
,
A_{2}+3A_{1}
,
2A_{2}+A_{1}
,
A_{3}+A_{1}
,
2A_{2}+2A_{1}
,

A_{3}+2A_{1}
,
D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{1}
,
A_{3}+A_{2}+A_{1}
,
A_{4}+A_{1}
,
2A_{3}
,
A_{4}+A_{3}
,
A_{5}+A_{1}
,
D_{5}(a_{1})+A_{2}

Three of these orbits are not rigid, namely:

A_{2}+A_{1},A_{4}+A_{1}\subset E_{7},\qquad A_{4}+A_{1}\subset E_{8}.

Proof.

In exceptional types, the cohomology groups
H^{2}(\mathbb{O},\mathbb{C})
were computed by Biswas and Chatterjee in
[
BC12
, Thms 5.11,5.12]
. It was shown there that
H^{2}(\mathbb{O},\mathbb{C})=0
in all cases except for the following nine orbits in type
E_{6}
:

2A_{1},A_{2}+A_{1},A_{2}+2A_{1},A_{3},A_{3}+A_{1},A_{4},A_{4}+A_{1},A_{5},D_{5}(a_{1}).

Thus by Proposition
3.8.1
,
\mathbb{O}
is birationally rigid if and only if
\mathbb{O}
is not one of these nine and all dimension 2 singularities in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
are of type
m
. Inspecting the diagrams in
[
FJLS15
, Sec 13]
, one arrives at the list given in the statement of the proposition. A list of rigid orbits in exceptional types is provided in
[
Els84
]
.
∎

3.9.
Classification of birationally semi-rigid orbits

For the calculations in Section
4
, birationally rigid nilpotent covers, and the orbits which admit them, will play a central role. Make the following definition.

Definition 3.9.1
.

A nilpotent cover
\mathbb{O}
is
birationally semi-rigid
if

(i)

\mathbb{O}
admits a
G
-equivariant birationally rigid cover.

(ii)

\mathbb{O}
is not birationally rigid.

Below, we will give a classification of such orbits in simple exceptional types. The following result from
[
LMM21
]
narrows the range of possibilities.

Proposition 3.9.2

(Prop 7.6.16,
[
LMM21
]
)
.

Suppose
\mathbb{O}
is a birationally semi-rigid orbit in a simple exceptional Lie algebra. Then all Kleinian singularities in
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
are of type
A_{1}
, with the following four exceptions:

(i)

\mathfrak{g}=E_{6}
and
\mathbb{O}=2A_{2}
. There is a unique codimension 2 leaf, and the corresponding singularity is of type
A_{2}
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{g}=E_{6}
and
\mathbb{O}=A_{5}
. There is a unique codimension 2 leaf, and the corresponding singularity is of type
A_{2}
.

(iii)

\mathfrak{g}=E_{6}
and
\mathbb{O}=E_{6}(a_{3})
. There are two codimension 2 leaves, and the corresponding singularities are of types
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
.

(iv)

\mathfrak{g}=E_{8}
and
\mathbb{O}=E_{8}(b_{6})
. There are two codimension 2 leaves, and the corresponding singularities are of types
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
.

In the simple exceptional Lie algebras, there are 38 nilpotent orbits with nontrivial
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})
which satisfy the
A_{1}
condition above. However, not all such orbits are birationally semi-rigid (the simplest example is the distinguished orbit
F_{4}(a_{2})
in
F_{4}
). Our task in this subsection is to determine precisely which of them are. For the most part, the techniques we will employ were developed in
[
LMM21
]
. We will review some of them here for the reader’s convenience.

If
\mathbb{O}
is a nilpotent orbit, consider the finite set

(3.9.1)

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O}):=\{(M,\mathbb{O}_{M})\mid\mathbb{O}=\Ind^{G}_{M}\mathbb{O}_{M},\ \mathbb{O}_{M}\text{ is rigid}\}/G.

Let
m(\mathbb{O})
denote the maximum value of
\dim\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{m})
for
(M,\mathbb{O}_{M})\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})
.

Lemma 3.9.3

(Prop 7.6.15,
[
LMM21
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}\subset\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
be a codimension 2 leaf. Let
\Sigma=\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
be the corresponding Kleinian singularity, and
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
the corresponding codimension two
G
-orbit. Assume:

(i)

\Gamma
is a simple group.

(ii)

There is a strict inequality

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})|^{-1}>m(\mathbb{O})

Then
\Sigma\subset\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
is smoothened under the covering map
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widehat{\mathbb{O}}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
.

Combining Lemma
3.9.3
and Proposition
3.7.1
, we obtain the following useful criterion.

Corollary 3.9.4
.

Suppose

(i)

All Kleinian singularities in
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
are of type
A_{1}
.

(ii)

For each codimension 2 orbit
\mathbb{O}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, there is a strict inequality

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}^{\prime})|^{-1}>m(\mathbb{O})

(iii)

The reductive part of the centralizer of
e\in\mathbb{O}
is semisimple.

Then the universal cover
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
of
\mathbb{O}
is birationally rigid.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9.3
, conditions (i) and (ii) imply that
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widehat{\mathbb{O}}])

has no codimension 2 leaves. By Lemma
3.6.1
, condition (iii) implies that
H^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{O}},\mathbb{C})=0
. The corollary follows at once from Proposition
3.7.1
.
∎

Proposition 3.9.5
.

The following is a complete list of birationally semi-rigid orbits in simple exceptional Lie algebras:

\mathfrak{g}

Birationally semi-rigid orbits

G_{2}

G_{2}(a_{1})

F_{4}

A_{2}
,
B_{2}
,
C_{3}(a_{1})
,
F_{4}(a_{3})
,

E_{6}

A_{2}
,
D_{4}(a_{1})
,
2A_{2}
,
A_{5}
,
E_{6}(a_{3})

E_{7}

(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{2}+3A_{1}
,
(A_{3}+A_{1})^{\prime\prime}
,
D_{4}(a_{1})
,
A_{3}+2A_{1}
,
D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{1}
,

A_{3}+A_{2}+A_{1}
,
A_{5}+A_{1}
,

,

E_{7}(a_{5})

E_{8}(a_{7})

E_{8}(b_{6})

Proof.

By Proposition
3.9.2
, we can restrict our attention to orbits with nontrivial
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})
and only
A_{1}
singularities in
\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])
. Using the incidence diagrams in
[
FJLS15
, Sec 13]
, we find that there are 38 orbits with these properties. For most of these orbits, Corollary
3.9.4
can be straightforwardly applied to show that
\mathbb{O}
is birationally semi-rigid. In some cases, a more elaborate argument is required. On the other hand, 10 of these orbits are
not
semi-rigid. This is proved either by cohomology considerations, see Proposition
3.7.1
, or by a counting argument involving Proposition
3.4.1
.

We pause to introduce some notational conventions which will remain in place for the rest of the paper. For
\mathfrak{g}
a simple exceptional Lie algebra, we number the simple roots as in Bourbaki (
[
Bou02
]
). In type
E_{n}
, this means that the simple roots forming the subgraph of type
A_{n-1}
are labeled
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3},...,\alpha_{n}
from left to right and the remaining simple root is labeled
\alpha_{2}
. In type
F_{4}
the simple roots are labeled
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{4}
from left to right (
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
are the long roots, and
\alpha_{3}
,
\alpha_{4}
are short). In type
G_{2}
,
\alpha_{1}
is the short root.

If
\mathfrak{g}
has rank
n
and
\{r_{1},...,r_{p}\}\subseteq\{1,...,n\}
, there is a unique standard Levi subalgebra with simple roots
\alpha_{r_{1}},...,\alpha_{r_{p}}
. We denote this Levi subalgebra by
\mathfrak{l}(X;r_{1},...,r_{p})
, where
X
is the Lie type of the Levi (
X
is included in the notation only for the reader’s convenience—it is completely determined by the numbers
r_{1},...,r_{p}
). Two standard Levis
\mathfrak{l}(X;r_{1},...,r_{p})
and
\mathfrak{l}(Y;s_{1},...,s_{q})
are conjugate under
\Ad(\mathfrak{g})
if and only if
p=q
and there is a Weyl group element
w
such that
\{\alpha_{r_{1}},...,\alpha_{r_{p}}\}=w\{\alpha_{s_{1}},...,\alpha_{s_{p}}\}
. Sometimes, the Levi subalgebra
\mathfrak{l}(X;r_{1},...,r_{p})
is completely determined by
X
. In such cases, we will often omit
r_{1},...,r_{p}
from the notation, writing simply
\mathfrak{l}(X)
.

The calculations below involve a number of elementary ‘micro-computations’, which are carried out in each case in exactly the same fashion. To avoid repeating references and explanations, we will catalogue them below:

•

Given a nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}
, determine the finite set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})
, see (
3.9.1
). This is deducible in every case from the tables in
[
DE09
, Sec 4]
.

•

Given a nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}
, determine the codimension 2 orbits
\mathbb{O}_{k}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. This is evident from the incidence diagrams in
[
Spa82
]
. In some cases, we will also need to determine the singularity of
\mathbb{O}_{k}
and its normalization,
\Sigma_{k}
. This can be deduced from the incidence diagrams in
[
FJLS15
, Sec 13]
.

•

Determine the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})
of a nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}
. See
[
CM93
, Sec 6.1]
for classical types and
[
CM93
, Sec 8.4]
for exceptional types.

•

Determine the reductive part
\mathfrak{r}
of the centralizer of a nilpotent element
e\in\mathbb{O}
. See
[
CM93
, Sec 6.1]
for classical types and
[
Car93
, Sec 13.1]
for exceptional types.

G_{2}(a_{1})\subset G_{2}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(A_{1};1),\{0\}),(L(A_{1};2),\{0\})\}

and therefore
m(\mathbb{O})=1
. There is one codimension 2 orbit in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=\widetilde{A}_{1}
. The fundamental groups are as follows

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=S_{3}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=1

Note that

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})|^{-1}=6>1=m(\mathbb{O}).

and
\mathfrak{r}=\{0\}
. So
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid by Corollary
3.9.4
.

A_{2}\subset F_{4}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(C_{3};2,3,4),\{0\}\},

and therefore
m(\mathbb{O})=1
. There is one codimension 2 orbit in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=A_{1}+\widetilde{A}_{1}
. The fundamental groups are as follows

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=S_{2}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=1

Note that

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})|^{-1}=2>1=m(\mathbb{O}),

and
\mathfrak{r}=A_{2}
. So
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid by Proposition
3.9.4
.

B_{2}\subset F_{4}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(C_{3};2,3,4),\mathbb{O}_{(2,1^{4})})\},

and therefore
m(\mathbb{O})=1
. There is one codimension 2 orbit in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=A_{2}+\widetilde{A}_{1}
. The fundamental groups are as follows

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=S_{2}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=1

Note that

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})|^{-1}=2>1=m(\mathbb{O}),

and
\mathfrak{r}=2A_{1}
. So
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid by Proposition
3.9.4
.

C_{3}(a_{1})\subset F_{4}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(B_{3};1,2,3),\mathbb{O}_{(2^{2},1^{3})})\},

and therefore
m(\mathbb{O})=1
. There are two codimension 2 orbits in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=B_{2}
and
\mathbb{O}_{2}=\widetilde{A}_{2}+A_{1}
. The singularity of
\mathbb{O}_{2}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}
is of type
m
and is therefore resolved under the normalization map
X=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. Let
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1}\subset X
be the preimage of
\mathbb{O}_{1}
. The slice
\Sigma_{1}
to
\mathbb{O}_{1}
is of type
2A_{1}
, and therefore the preimage of the
\Sigma_{1}
is the disjoint union of two copies of
\Sigma_{1}
. If
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1}
is not connected, then there are at least two symplectic leaves of codimension
2
over
\mathbb{O}_{1}
with open
G
orbits being the irreducible components of
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1}
. That contradicts to
Lemma

3.2.2
. It follows that
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1}
is a
2
-fold connected cover of
\mathbb{O}_{1}
. The fundamental groups are as follows

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=S_{2}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=S_{2}\qquad\pi_{1}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1})=1

Note that

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1})|^{-1}=2>1=m(\mathbb{O}),

and
\mathfrak{r}=A_{1}
. Analogously to Proposition
3.9.4
, we see that
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid.

F_{4}(a_{3})\subset F_{4}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(A_{1}+A_{2};1,3,4),\{0\}),(L(A_{2}+A_{1};1,2,4),\{0\}),(L(B_{2}),\{0\})\},

and therefore
m(\mathbb{O})=2
. There is one codimension 2 orbit in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=C_{3}(a_{1})
. The fundamental groups are as follows

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=S_{4}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=S_{2}

Note that

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})|^{-1}=12>2=m(\mathbb{O}),

and
\mathfrak{r}=0
. So
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid by Proposition
3.9.4
.

F_{4}(a_{2})\subset F_{4}
. Note that
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=S_{2}
. Hence,
\mathbb{O}
admits 2 non-isomorphic covers (including the trivial one). We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(2A_{1};1,4),\{0\}),(L(B_{2};2,3),\mathbb{O}_{(2^{2},1)})\}.

By (iii) of Proposition
3.4.1
,
\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L(2A_{1};1,4)}\{0\}
and
\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L(B_{2};2,3)}\mathbb{O}_{(2^{2},1)}
are non-isomorphic covers of
\mathbb{O}
. In particular, all covers of
\mathbb{O}
are birationally induced.

A_{2}\subset E_{6}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(A_{5}),\{0\})\},

and therefore

. There is one codimension 2 orbit in

, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=3A_{1}
. The fundamental groups are as follows

. So
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}

If
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a birationally induced cover of
\mathbb{O}
, then
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{M}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
for some pair

and
M
-equivariant cover
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M}
of
\mathbb{O}_{M}
. By the table above, there are 5 such
(M,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
, up to conjugation by
G
. However, the pairs
(L(D_{5}),\mathbb{O}_{(3^{2},1^{4})}),(L(A_{4}+A_{1}),\mathbb{O}_{(2,1^{3})}\times\{0\}),(L(A_{5}),\mathbb{O}_{(2^{2},1^{2})})\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{O})
are induced from a common element of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})
, namely
(L(A_{3}+A_{1};1,2,4,5),\{0\})
. Hence three of the five
(M,\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{M})
give rise to isomorphic covers of
\mathbb{O}
. It follows that there are at most 3 non-isomorphic birationally induced covers of
\mathbb{O}
. Since
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})\simeq S_{3}
, there are a total of 4 non-isomorphic covers of
\mathbb{O}
. So at least one such is birationally rigid.

(3A_{1})^{\prime\prime}\subset E_{7}

(A_{3}+A_{1})^{\prime\prime}\subset E_{7}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(D_{5}),\{0\})\},

and therefore

. There are two codimension 2 orbits in

, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=A_{3}
, and

. The fundamental groups are as follows

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=1\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{2})=1.

Let
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
be the universal cover of
\mathbb{O}
.

We will show that the singularity
\Sigma_{1}
of
X
is resolved under the map
\widehat{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
. Note that
|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})|^{-1}=2=m(\mathbb{O})
. So if
\Sigma_{1}
is not resolved, both
\mathbb{O}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
are birationally induced from a co-rank 2 Levi. Since
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})
contains a single Levi (of co-rank 2), there is only one cover of
\mathbb{O}
which can be induced from a corank 2 Levi, namely
\mathrm{Bind}_{L}^{G}(\{0\})
. We conclude that
\Sigma_{1}
is resolved under the map
\widehat{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
. Analogously,
\Sigma_{2}
is resolved.
We have
\mathfrak{r}=B_{3}
, and therefore
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid by Corollary
3.9.4
.

D_{4}(a_{1})\subset E_{7}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(A_{1}+A_{5}),\{0\}),(L(D_{6}),\mathbb{O}_{(2^{4},1^{4})})\},

. There is one codimension 2 orbit in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=(A_{3}+A_{1})^{\prime}
. The fundamental groups are as follows

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=S_{3}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=1.

Note that

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})|^{-1}=6>1=m(\mathbb{O}),

. So
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}

3.9.4
.

A_{3}+2A_{1}\subset E_{7}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(E_{6}),3A_{1})\},

and therefore

. There is one codimension 2 orbit in

, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=(A_{3}+A_{1})^{\prime}
. The fundamental groups are as follows

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=1.

Note that

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})|^{-1}=2>1=m(\mathbb{O}),

D_{4}(a_{1})+A_{1}\subset E_{7}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(A_{5};1,3,4,5,6),\{0\})\},

and therefore

. There are two codimension 2 orbits in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=D_{4}(a_{1})
and
\mathbb{O}_{2}=A_{3}+2A_{1}
. Consider the map
X=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{O}])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\overline{\mathbb{O}}
. Let
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1}\subset X
be the preimage of
\mathbb{O}_{1}
. The slice
\Sigma_{1}
to
\mathbb{O}_{1}
is of type
3A_{1}
, and therefore the preimage of
\Sigma_{1}
is a disjoint union of three copies of
A_{1}
singularities. The singularity
\Sigma_{2}
of the leaf
\mathfrak{L}_{2}
corresponding to
\mathbb{O}_{2}
is of type
A_{1}
. If
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1}
is not connected, then there are at least two symplectic leaves of codimension
2
over
\mathbb{O}_{1}
with open
G
orbits being the irreducible components of
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1}
. That contradicts to
Lemma

3.2.2
. It follows that
\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1}
is a
3
-fold connected cover of
\mathbb{O}_{1}
. The fundamental groups are as follows

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=S_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=S_{3}\qquad\pi_{1}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{2})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}

Note that

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\widecheck{\mathbb{O}}_{1})|^{-1}=2=m(\mathbb{O}),\qquad|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}({\mathbb{O}}_{2})|^{-1}=2=m(\mathbb{O}).

Let
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathbb{O}
be the universal cover of
\mathbb{O}
. If one of the singularities
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}
is not resolved under
\widehat{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
, then we have
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{X}=2
and
\dim\mathfrak{P}^{\widehat{X}}\geq 2
. Thus, both
\mathbb{O}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
are birationally induced from a corank
2
Levi. However, there is only one cover that can be induced from a corank
2
Levi, namely
\mathrm{Bind}_{L}^{G}(\{0\})
. Therefore, both
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are resolved under
\widehat{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}X
.
We have
\mathfrak{r}=2A_{1}
, and therefore
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid by Corollary
3.9.4
.

A_{3}+A_{2}\subset E_{7}
. Note that
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=S_{2}
. Hence,
\mathbb{O}
has two non-isomorphic covers (including the trivial one). We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(A_{1}+D_{5}),\{0\}\times\mathbb{O}_{(2^{2},1^{6})}),(L(D_{6}),\mathbb{O}_{(3,2^{2},1^{5})})\}

3.4.1
,
\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L(A_{1}+D_{5})}\{0\}
and
\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L(D_{6})}\mathbb{O}_{(3,2^{2},1^{5})}

\mathbb{O}
. In particular, all covers of
\mathbb{O}
are birationally induced.

A_{3}+A_{2}+A_{1}\subset E_{7}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(A_{4}+A_{2};1,2,3,4,6,7),\{0\})\}.

Since
L=L(A_{4}+A_{2};1,2,3,4,6,7)
is of semisimple co-rank 1 and
\mathbb{O}_{L}=\{0\}
, there is a unique birationally induced cover of
\mathbb{O}
, namely
\mathrm{Bind}_{L}^{G}\{0\}
. On the other hand, since
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, there are two non-isomorphic covers of
\mathbb{O}
. So one must be birationally rigid.

D_{4}+A_{1}\subset E_{7}
. Note that
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Hence,
\mathbb{O}
has two non-isomorphic covers (including the trivial one). We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(D_{6}),\mathbb{O}_{(3,2^{4},1)})\}

Write
(L,\mathbb{O}_{L})=(L(D_{6}),\mathbb{O}_{(3,2^{4},1)})
. An
atlas
computation shows that
\pi_{1}^{L}(\mathbb{O}_{L})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
denote the 2-fold

-equivariant cover of
\mathbb{O}_{L}
. By (iv) of Proposition
3.4.1
,
\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}_{L}
is a 2-fold cover of
\mathrm{Bind}^{G}_{L}\mathbb{O}_{L}
. In particular, both covers of
\mathbb{O}
are birationally induced.

A_{5}+A_{1}\subset E_{7}
. We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(E_{6}),2A_{2}+A_{1})\},

and therefore
m(\mathbb{O})=1
. There is one codimension 2 orbit in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}
, namely
\mathbb{O}_{1}=A_{4}+A_{2}
. The fundamental groups are as follows

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\qquad\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})=1.

Note that

|\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})||\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O}_{1})|^{-1}=2>1=m(\mathbb{O}),

and

. So
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
is birationally rigid by Corollary
3.9.4
.

D_{5}(a_{1})+A_{1}\subset E_{7}
. Note that
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{O})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Hence,
\mathbb{O}
has two non-isomorphic covers (including the trivial one). We have

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathbb{O})=\{(L(A_{3}+A_{2}),\{0\})\}

We note that the induction from
(L(A_{3}+A_{2}),\{0\})
is birational (there are several ways to see this — one will be given in the
D_{5}(a_{1})+A_{1}\subset E_{7}
portion of Section
4.3
). Recall the set
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{O})
defined above. Since
\mathbb{O}
is birationally induced from
(L(A_{3}+A_{2}),\{0\})
, every pair
(M,\mathbb{O}_{M})\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{O})
is of the form
\mathbb{O}_{M}=\Ind^{M}_{L}\{0\}
, where
M
is a co-rank 1 Levi containing (a
G
-conjugate of)

D_{5}+A_{2}\subset E_{8}
. Recall that

. The singularity of a codimension

is not of type

(namely, it is of type

), and therefore the Namikawa space
\mathfrak{P}^{X}
for

is non-trivial. Thus,
\mathbb{O}
is birationally induced, and so all covers of
\mathbb{O}
are birationally induced.

D_{7}(a_{1})\subset E_{8}
. Since there is a cosimension
2
orbit

with the singularity not of type
